Q1   [20]
CO-1 BL-   a   Concept of Cost Accounting:   (5)
Rememb
Cost Accounting is the branch of accounting that deals with the identification, measurement,
er
allocation, aggregation, analysis, and presentation of costs of products, services, activities,
and cost centres for the purpose of planning, control, and decision-making by management.
Objectives:
1. Ascertainment of Cost: To determine the cost per unit of output, process, or service.
2. Cost Control: To compare actual costs with standards/budgets and take corrective action.
3. Cost Reduction: To achieve real and permanent reduction in cost per unit.
4. Price Fixation: To help management fix a remunerative selling price.
5. Decision-making: To provide cost data for make-or-buy, product mix, pricing, and shut-
down decisions.
Usefulness to Management:
Cost Accounting provides timely, detailed, product-wise cost data that financial accounting
cannot. It enables management to price products correctly, identify loss-making product
lines, control wastage of materials and labour, and plan for future operations.
Key Differences between Cost Accounting and Financial Accounting:
Basis   Financial Accounting   Cost Accounting
Purpose   Statutory reporting to   Cost ascertainment and
external users   control — for internal use

CO-4 BL-   c   Types of Labour Variances:   (5)
Rememb
A Labour Variance is the difference between the standard labour cost for actual output and
er
the actual labour cost incurred. The three principal labour variances are:
Variance   Formula   Reasons / Causes
1. Labour Cost Variance   (SH × SR) – (AH × AR)   Overall difference between
(LCV)   standard and actual labour cost.
Analyses total efficiency of labour
utilisation.
2. Labour Rate Variance   AH × (SR – AR)   Change in wage rates (union
(LRV)   settlements, overtime premium,
new labour laws, change in skill
mix). Usually uncontrollable at
supervisor level.
3. Labour Efficiency Variance SR × (SH – AH)   Difference in hours worked versus
(LEV)   standard hours for actual output.
Caused by worker skill, machine
breakdown, poor supervision,
faulty material.
Significance: Labour Rate Variance is generally a planning/personnel issue; Labour Efficiency
Variance is an operational/supervisor's responsibility. Splitting LCV into LRV and LEV enables
'Management by Exception' — only the significant variances are investigated.

Minimum Level   ROL – (Normal Usage × Normal Lead   3,900 – (450 × 5) = 3,900 – 2,250

Time)   – 720 = 6,680 units
Average Stock Level   (Min Level + Max Level) ÷ 2   (1,650 + 6,680) ÷ 2 = 4,165 units
Annual Demand (A) = Normal Usage × 52 weeks = 450 × 52 = 23,400 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 200 per order
Carrying Cost (C) = 20% × ₹ 50 = ₹ 10 per unit per year
EOQ = √(2 × A × O ÷ C) = √(2 × 23,400 × 200 ÷ 10) = √(93,60,000 ÷ 10) = √9,36,000 ≈ 967
units
Q2   [20]
CO-2 BL-   a   Classification of Costs by Controllability:   (5)
Rememb
Costs can be classified on the basis of the degree to which they can be influenced by the
er
action of a specified member of management. The classification distinguishes between costs
a manager can control and those he cannot.
1. Controllable Costs:
Costs that can be influenced by the action of a specified individual or manager during a
defined period. Usually variable costs fall in this category.
Examples: Direct materials, direct labour, power consumption, factory supplies, overtime
wages. A production foreman can control material wastage and overtime.
2. Uncontrollable (Non-controllable) Costs:
Costs that cannot be influenced by a specific manager in the short term — either because
they are fixed by contract, policy, or external factors. Usually fixed and policy costs.
Examples: Rent of factory, insurance premium, depreciation of plant, salary of the Managing
Director (at a supervisor's level), property tax.
Note: Controllability is relative — a cost uncontrollable at a foreman's level may be
controllable at the CEO's level, and what is uncontrollable in the short term may be
controllable in the long term.
Escapable vs. Inescapable Costs:
Type   Meaning   Example
Escapable Cost   Costs that can be avoided if a   Direct material cost of a
particular course of action (e.g.,   discontinued product line.
shutting down a department) is taken.
Inescapable Cost   Costs that continue to be incurred   Rent of a leased factory; salary
even if the activity is stopped   of permanent staff.
(committed costs).

CO-3 BL-   b   Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB):   (5)
Understa
Zero-Based Budgeting is a budgeting technique in which every item of expenditure must be
nd
justified from a 'zero base' each year — as though it were a brand new activity. Managers are
required to analyse their departmental needs and costs without reference to previous years'
budgets. Introduced by Peter Pyhrr (Texas Instruments, 1970) and popularised in US
government in the 1970s.
ZBB vs. Traditional (Incremental) Budgeting:
Basis   Traditional Budgeting   Zero-Based Budgeting
Starting point   Last year's actual/budgeted figures +   Zero — every rupee must be
an increment   justified afresh
Focus   Changes from previous period (inputs)   Value for money / outputs and
decision packages
Assumption   Past activities are worth continuing   No activity is continued
automatically
Effort required   Low   High — detailed cost-benefit
analysis
Advantages of ZBB: (i) eliminates wasteful expenditure, (ii) fosters cost consciousness and
critical review, (iii) links budgets to corporate objectives and priorities.
Limitations: (i) time-consuming and paperwork-intensive; (ii) requires skilled managers and
may face resistance from staff.

Apply

t   ks
Given: Annual Demand (A) = 3,600 units │ Ordering Cost (O) = ₹120 per order │ Regular Price
= ₹60 per unit │ Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. = 0.25 × 60 = ₹15 per unit per year
Supplier offers 5% discount if order size ≥ 800 units → Discounted Price = ₹60 × 0.95 =
₹57/unit; Carrying Cost at discounted price = 0.25 × 57 = ₹14.25/unit
Step 1 — EOQ at the Regular Price:
EOQ = √(2 × A × O ÷ C) = √(2 × 3,600 × 120 ÷ 15) = √(8,64,000 ÷ 15) = √57,600 = 240 units ✓
Step 2 — Total Annual Cost Comparison (including Purchase Cost):
Cost Element   At EOQ (240 units)   At 800 units (5% discount)
Purchase price per unit   ₹ 60.00   ₹ 57.00
No. of orders per year   3,600 ÷ 240 = 15 orders   3,600 ÷ 800 = 4.5 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   15 × 120 = ₹ 1,800   4.5 × 120 = ₹ 540
Average Inventory   240 ÷ 2 = 120 units   800 ÷ 2 = 400 units
Annual Carrying Cost   120 × ₹15 = ₹ 1,800   400 × ₹14.25 = ₹ 5,700
Annual Purchase Cost   3,600 × 60 = ₹ 2,16,000   3,600 × 57 = ₹ 2,05,200
TOTAL ANNUAL COST   ₹ 2,19,600   ₹ 2,11,440
Annual Saving from availing discount = ₹ 2,19,600 – ₹ 2,11,440 = ₹ 8,160
Recommendation: The company SHOULD avail the 5% quantity discount. The reduction in
purchase price (₹ 10,800) more than offsets the increase in carrying cost (₹ 3,900), yielding a
net annual saving of ₹ 8,160.
CO-5 BL-   b   ABC Analysis (Always Better Control) [8 Marks]   (8)
Apply
ABC Analysis is a selective inventory control technique based on the Pareto Principle (the
80:20 rule). It classifies inventory items into three categories — A, B, and C — on the basis of
their annual consumption value (Usage × Price), and prescribes different levels of
management control and attention for each category.
Classification Pattern:
Category   % of Items   % of Value   Control Approach
A — Most important About 10% of items   About 70% of value   Tight control:
frequent review, lo
safety stock, EOQ,
strict supervision
B — Moderately   About 20% of items   About 20% of value   Moderate control:
important   periodic review,
moderate safety
stock
C — Least important About 70% of items   About 10% of value   Loose control: bulk
ordering, high safe
stock, two-bin
system
Example: An automobile assembly plant may have:
• Category A: Engine, gearbox, body panels — few in number but costly; closely monitored.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
• Category B: Tyres, batteries, wiring harness — moderate value and frequency.
• Category C: Bolts, clips, paint, fasteners — many in number but individually inexpensive;
purchased in bulk.
Benefits to the Store Manager:
1. Selective attention — management effort is focused where it matters most.
2. Reduction in investment in inventory without disrupting operations.
3. Tighter control on A-items reduces pilferage, obsolescence, and stock-outs of valuable
materials.
4. Simplifies purchasing procedures for low-value C-items (bulk buying reduces ordering cost).

CO-3 BL-   b   Master Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
Apply
A Master Budget is a comprehensive, consolidated statement that summarises all functional
(subsidiary) budgets of an organisation into one unified plan for a defined future period. It
reflects the final approved budget of the whole organisation and is typically approved at the
board level.
Components of a Master Budget:
1. Operating Budgets: Sales Budget, Production Budget, Raw Material Purchase Budget,
Direct Labour Budget, Factory Overhead Budget, Selling and Administration Budget.
2. Financial Budgets: Cash Budget, Budgeted Profit & Loss Account, Budgeted Balance Sheet,
Capital Expenditure Budget.
Significance:
• It is the primary tool of integrated corporate planning — ensures that all departments move
in the same direction.
• It quantifies the firm's goals in monetary terms and projects the financial outcome of
operations.
• It acts as the benchmark against which actual results are compared for variance analysis
and corrective action.
• It facilitates coordination between departments — the sales budget drives production,
production drives purchasing, and so on.

CO-3 BL-   b   Marginal Costing vs. Absorption Costing [8 Marks]   (8)
Understa
Basis   Marginal Costing   Absorption Costing
nd
Treatment of Fixed OH   Treated as a period cost — charged in Treated as a product cost — include
full to the P&L of the period   in cost of production and inventory
Inventory Valuation   Valued at variable cost only   Valued at total (variable + fixed)
factory cost
Cost Statement Focus   Contribution (Sales – Variable Cost)   Gross Profit (Sales – Cost of Goods
Sold)
Purpose   Short-term decision-making, CVP   Statutory reporting (required under
analysis, pricing   AS/Ind AS)
Effect on Profit   Profit depends only on sales volume   Profit depends on both sales and
production volume
When Will the Profits Differ?
Profits reported under the two methods differ whenever there is a change in inventory levels
(opening stock ≠ closing stock), because the fixed overheads absorbed in stock are deferred
to later periods in absorption costing but written off immediately in marginal costing.
Situation   Effect under Absorption Costing   Direction of Differenc
Production > Sales (stock ↑)   Part of fixed OH is carried forward in   Absorption profit > M
closing stock
Production < Sales (stock ↓)   Fixed OH of past periods is released in   Absorption profit < M
current period
Production = Sales (stock constant)   No deferred fixed OH   Both methods give th
Example: If fixed OH per unit is ₹ 10 and closing stock exceeds opening stock by 1,000 units,
absorption costing reports ₹ 10,000 more profit than marginal costing for the same sales.

CO-3 BL-   b   Flexible Budget – Concept and Cost Classification   (5)
Understa
A Flexible Budget is a budget prepared to show expected costs and revenues at
nd
different levels of activity or capacity utilisation. Unlike a fixed budget, which
is prepared for only one level of output, a flexible budget adjusts according to
changes in production volume, making it a more realistic and practical tool for
managerial decision-making.
It is particularly useful in industries where output levels fluctuate due to
demand variability, seasonal factors, or operational constraints.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Concept of Flexible Budget
The fundamental idea behind a flexible budget is that cost behaviour changes
with the level of activity. Therefore, instead of having a single static estimate,
multiple budgeted figures are prepared for different activity levels (e.g., 60%,
80%, 100% capacity).
This enables management to:
• Compare actual performance with the appropriate budget level
•   Control costs more effectively
• Improve planning accuracy
Classification of Costs in Flexible Budgeting
For preparing a flexible budget, costs are classified based on their behaviour
with respect to changes in activity levels:
1. Fixed Costs
• These costs remain constant in total regardless of the level of production
within a relevant range.
•   Per unit fixed cost decreases as output increases.
•   Examples: Factory rent, salaries of permanent staff, insurance.
2. Variable Costs
• These costs vary directly and proportionately with the level of output.
•   Per unit variable cost remains constant.
•   Examples: Direct materials, direct labour (in many cases), power used in
production.
3. Semi-Variable (Mixed) Costs
• These costs contain both fixed and variable components.
• They partly change with output but not in direct proportion.
•   Examples: Electricity bills (fixed charge + usage charge), maintenance costs,
telephone expenses.
Importance of Cost Classification
Proper classification of costs is critical in flexible budgeting for the following
reasons:
1. Accurate Budget Preparation
It ensures that costs are adjusted correctly according to changes in activity
levels.
2. Better Cost Control
Management can identify which costs should vary and which should remain
stable, helping in variance analysis.
3. Improved Decision-Making
Helps in decisions related to pricing, production levels, and resource
allocation.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
4. Realistic Performance Evaluation
Actual results can be compared with the budget corresponding to the actual
level of activity, avoiding misleading conclusions.
5. Efficient Resource Utilisation
Enables better planning and utilisation of resources under different operating
conditions.
Conclusion
A flexible budget is an essential tool in modern cost accounting as it reflects
the dynamic nature of business operations. The correct classification of costs
into fixed, variable, and semi-variable forms the backbone of this budgeting
technique, ensuring accuracy, control, and informed managerial decisions.
********** 13 **********
````

Q1   [20]
CO-1 BL-   a   Concept of Cost Accounting:   (5)
Rememb
Cost Accounting is the branch of accounting that deals with the identification, measurement,
er
allocation, aggregation, analysis, and presentation of costs of products, services, activities,
and cost centres for the purpose of planning, control, and decision-making by management.
Objectives:
1. Ascertainment of Cost: To determine the cost per unit of output, process, or service.
2. Cost Control: To compare actual costs with standards/budgets and take corrective action.
3. Cost Reduction: To achieve real and permanent reduction in cost per unit.
4. Price Fixation: To help management fix a remunerative selling price.
5. Decision-making: To provide cost data for make-or-buy, product mix, pricing, and shut-
down decisions.
Usefulness to Management:
Cost Accounting provides timely, detailed, product-wise cost data that financial accounting
cannot. It enables management to price products correctly, identify loss-making product
lines, control wastage of materials and labour, and plan for future operations.
Key Differences between Cost Accounting and Financial Accounting:
Basis   Financial Accounting   Cost Accounting
Purpose   Statutory reporting to   Cost ascertainment and
external users   control — for internal use

and Balance Sheet   process-wise, department-

Companies Act   specified industries

CO-4 BL-   c   Types of Labour Variances:   (5)
Rememb
A Labour Variance is the difference between the standard labour cost for actual output and
er
the actual labour cost incurred. The three principal labour variances are:
Variance   Formula   Reasons / Causes
1. Labour Cost Variance   (SH × SR) – (AH × AR)   Overall difference between
(LCV)   standard and actual labour cost.
Analyses total efficiency of labour
utilisation.
2. Labour Rate Variance   AH × (SR – AR)   Change in wage rates (union
(LRV)   settlements, overtime premium,
new labour laws, change in skill
mix). Usually uncontrollable at
supervisor level.
3. Labour Efficiency Variance SR × (SH – AH)   Difference in hours worked versus
(LEV)   standard hours for actual output.
Caused by worker skill, machine
breakdown, poor supervision,
faulty material.
Significance: Labour Rate Variance is generally a planning/personnel issue; Labour Efficiency
Variance is an operational/supervisor's responsibility. Splitting LCV into LRV and LEV enables
'Management by Exception' — only the significant variances are investigated.

Q2   [20]
CO-2 BL-   a   Classification of Costs by Controllability:   (5)
Rememb
Costs can be classified on the basis of the degree to which they can be influenced by the
er
action of a specified member of management. The classification distinguishes between costs
a manager can control and those he cannot.
1. Controllable Costs:
Costs that can be influenced by the action of a specified individual or manager during a
defined period. Usually variable costs fall in this category.
Examples: Direct materials, direct labour, power consumption, factory supplies, overtime
wages. A production foreman can control material wastage and overtime.
2. Uncontrollable (Non-controllable) Costs:
Costs that cannot be influenced by a specific manager in the short term — either because
they are fixed by contract, policy, or external factors. Usually fixed and policy costs.
Examples: Rent of factory, insurance premium, depreciation of plant, salary of the Managing
Director (at a supervisor's level), property tax.
Note: Controllability is relative — a cost uncontrollable at a foreman's level may be
controllable at the CEO's level, and what is uncontrollable in the short term may be
controllable in the long term.
Escapable vs. Inescapable Costs:
Type   Meaning   Example
Escapable Cost   Costs that can be avoided if a   Direct material cost of a
particular course of action (e.g.,   discontinued product line.
shutting down a department) is taken.
Inescapable Cost   Costs that continue to be incurred   Rent of a leased factory; salary
even if the activity is stopped   of permanent staff.
(committed costs).

CO-3 BL-   b   Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB):   (5)
Understa
Zero-Based Budgeting is a budgeting technique in which every item of expenditure must be
nd
justified from a 'zero base' each year — as though it were a brand new activity. Managers are
required to analyse their departmental needs and costs without reference to previous years'
budgets. Introduced by Peter Pyhrr (Texas Instruments, 1970) and popularised in US
government in the 1970s.
ZBB vs. Traditional (Incremental) Budgeting:
Basis   Traditional Budgeting   Zero-Based Budgeting
Starting point   Last year's actual/budgeted figures +   Zero — every rupee must be
an increment   justified afresh
Focus   Changes from previous period (inputs)   Value for money / outputs and
decision packages
Assumption   Past activities are worth continuing   No activity is continued
automatically
Effort required   Low   High — detailed cost-benefit
analysis
Advantages of ZBB: (i) eliminates wasteful expenditure, (ii) fosters cost consciousness and
critical review, (iii) links budgets to corporate objectives and priorities.
Limitations: (i) time-consuming and paperwork-intensive; (ii) requires skilled managers and
may face resistance from staff.

Given: Annual Demand (A) = 3,600 units │ Ordering Cost (O) = ₹120 per order │ Regular Price
= ₹60 per unit │ Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. = 0.25 × 60 = ₹15 per unit per year
Supplier offers 5% discount if order size ≥ 800 units → Discounted Price = ₹60 × 0.95 =
₹57/unit; Carrying Cost at discounted price = 0.25 × 57 = ₹14.25/unit
Step 1 — EOQ at the Regular Price:
EOQ = √(2 × A × O ÷ C) = √(2 × 3,600 × 120 ÷ 15) = √(8,64,000 ÷ 15) = √57,600 = 240 units ✓
Step 2 — Total Annual Cost Comparison (including Purchase Cost):
Cost Element   At EOQ (240 units)   At 800 units (5% discount)
Purchase price per unit   ₹ 60.00   ₹ 57.00
No. of orders per year   3,600 ÷ 240 = 15 orders   3,600 ÷ 800 = 4.5 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   15 × 120 = ₹ 1,800   4.5 × 120 = ₹ 540
Average Inventory   240 ÷ 2 = 120 units   800 ÷ 2 = 400 units
Annual Carrying Cost   120 × ₹15 = ₹ 1,800   400 × ₹14.25 = ₹ 5,700
Annual Purchase Cost   3,600 × 60 = ₹ 2,16,000   3,600 × 57 = ₹ 2,05,200
TOTAL ANNUAL COST   ₹ 2,19,600   ₹ 2,11,440
Annual Saving from availing discount = ₹ 2,19,600 – ₹ 2,11,440 = ₹ 8,160
Recommendation: The company SHOULD avail the 5% quantity discount. The reduction in
purchase price (₹ 10,800) more than offsets the increase in carrying cost (₹ 3,900), yielding a
net annual saving of ₹ 8,160.
CO-5 BL-   b   ABC Analysis (Always Better Control) [8 Marks]   (8)
Apply
ABC Analysis is a selective inventory control technique based on the Pareto Principle (the
80:20 rule). It classifies inventory items into three categories — A, B, and C — on the basis of
their annual consumption value (Usage × Price), and prescribes different levels of
management control and attention for each category.
Classification Pattern:
Category   % of Items   % of Value   Control Approach
A — Most important About 10% of items   About 70% of value   Tight control:
frequent review, lo
safety stock, EOQ,
strict supervision
B — Moderately   About 20% of items   About 20% of value   Moderate control:
important   periodic review,
moderate safety
stock
C — Least important About 70% of items   About 10% of value   Loose control: bulk
ordering, high safe
stock, two-bin
system
Example: An automobile assembly plant may have:
• Category A: Engine, gearbox, body panels — few in number but costly; closely monitored.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
• Category B: Tyres, batteries, wiring harness — moderate value and frequency.
• Category C: Bolts, clips, paint, fasteners — many in number but individually inexpensive;
purchased in bulk.
Benefits to the Store Manager:
1. Selective attention — management effort is focused where it matters most.
2. Reduction in investment in inventory without disrupting operations.
3. Tighter control on A-items reduces pilferage, obsolescence, and stock-outs of valuable
materials.
4. Simplifies purchasing procedures for low-value C-items (bulk buying reduces ordering cost).

CO-3 BL-   b   Master Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
Apply
A Master Budget is a comprehensive, consolidated statement that summarises all functional
(subsidiary) budgets of an organisation into one unified plan for a defined future period. It
reflects the final approved budget of the whole organisation and is typically approved at the
board level.
Components of a Master Budget:
1. Operating Budgets: Sales Budget, Production Budget, Raw Material Purchase Budget,
Direct Labour Budget, Factory Overhead Budget, Selling and Administration Budget.
2. Financial Budgets: Cash Budget, Budgeted Profit & Loss Account, Budgeted Balance Sheet,
Capital Expenditure Budget.
Significance:
• It is the primary tool of integrated corporate planning — ensures that all departments move
in the same direction.
• It quantifies the firm's goals in monetary terms and projects the financial outcome of
operations.
• It acts as the benchmark against which actual results are compared for variance analysis
and corrective action.
• It facilitates coordination between departments — the sales budget drives production,
production drives purchasing, and so on.

CO-3 BL-   b   Marginal Costing vs. Absorption Costing [8 Marks]   (8)
Understa
Basis   Marginal Costing   Absorption Costing
nd
Treatment of Fixed OH   Treated as a period cost — charged in Treated as a product cost — include
full to the P&L of the period   in cost of production and inventory
Inventory Valuation   Valued at variable cost only   Valued at total (variable + fixed)
factory cost
Cost Statement Focus   Contribution (Sales – Variable Cost)   Gross Profit (Sales – Cost of Goods
Sold)
Purpose   Short-term decision-making, CVP   Statutory reporting (required under
analysis, pricing   AS/Ind AS)
Effect on Profit   Profit depends only on sales volume   Profit depends on both sales and
production volume
When Will the Profits Differ?
Profits reported under the two methods differ whenever there is a change in inventory levels
(opening stock ≠ closing stock), because the fixed overheads absorbed in stock are deferred
to later periods in absorption costing but written off immediately in marginal costing.
Situation   Effect under Absorption Costing   Direction of Differenc
Production > Sales (stock ↑)   Part of fixed OH is carried forward in   Absorption profit > M
closing stock
Production < Sales (stock ↓)   Fixed OH of past periods is released in   Absorption profit < M
current period

CO-3 BL-   b   Flexible Budget – Concept and Cost Classification   (5)
Understa
A Flexible Budget is a budget prepared to show expected costs and revenues at
nd
different levels of activity or capacity utilisation. Unlike a fixed budget, which
is prepared for only one level of output, a flexible budget adjusts according to
changes in production volume, making it a more realistic and practical tool for
managerial decision-making.
It is particularly useful in industries where output levels fluctuate due to
demand variability, seasonal factors, or operational constraints.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Concept of Flexible Budget
The fundamental idea behind a flexible budget is that cost behaviour changes
with the level of activity. Therefore, instead of having a single static estimate,
multiple budgeted figures are prepared for different activity levels (e.g., 60%,
80%, 100% capacity).
This enables management to:
• Compare actual performance with the appropriate budget level
•   Control costs more effectively
• Improve planning accuracy
Classification of Costs in Flexible Budgeting
For preparing a flexible budget, costs are classified based on their behaviour
with respect to changes in activity levels:
1. Fixed Costs
• These costs remain constant in total regardless of the level of production
within a relevant range.
•   Per unit fixed cost decreases as output increases.
•   Examples: Factory rent, salaries of permanent staff, insurance.
2. Variable Costs
• These costs vary directly and proportionately with the level of output.
•   Per unit variable cost remains constant.
•   Examples: Direct materials, direct labour (in many cases), power used in
production.
3. Semi-Variable (Mixed) Costs
• These costs contain both fixed and variable components.
• They partly change with output but not in direct proportion.
•   Examples: Electricity bills (fixed charge + usage charge), maintenance costs,
telephone expenses.
Importance of Cost Classification
Proper classification of costs is critical in flexible budgeting for the following
reasons:
1. Accurate Budget Preparation
It ensures that costs are adjusted correctly according to changes in activity
levels.
2. Better Cost Control
Management can identify which costs should vary and which should remain
stable, helping in variance analysis.
3. Improved Decision-Making
Helps in decisions related to pricing, production levels, and resource
allocation.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
4. Realistic Performance Evaluation
Actual results can be compared with the budget corresponding to the actual
level of activity, avoiding misleading conclusions.
5. Efficient Resource Utilisation
Enables better planning and utilisation of resources under different operating
conditions.
Conclusion
A flexible budget is an essential tool in modern cost accounting as it reflects
the dynamic nature of business operations. The correct classification of costs
into fixed, variable, and semi-variable forms the backbone of this budgeting
technique, ensuring accuracy, control, and informed managerial decisions.
********** 13 **********
````

Q1
a.   1. Revenue Recognition Principle:
Definition: Revenue is recognized when it is earned and the right to receive payment is
established, not when cash is received.
Purpose: To ensure income is recorded in the correct accounting period, regardless of
when money is received.
Example: A business sells goods worth ₹1,00,000 on credit in March 2025, and receives
payment in April 2025.
According to the Revenue Recognition Principle, revenue should be recorded in March,
when the sale occurred.
2. Matching Concept:
Definition: Expenses should be recorded in the same accounting period as the
revenues they help generate.
Purpose: To correctly determine profit or loss for a specific period.
Example: If goods sold in March 2025 cost ₹60,000, this expense should also be
recorded in March 2025, even if the goods were purchased earlier.

d.   Accounting information is used by a wide range of individuals and organizations to
make informed decisions. These users are generally classified into two categories:
internal users and external users.
External Users:
These are individuals or institutions outside the organization who need financial
information to assess the firm’s performance.
a) Investors / Shareholders
Use financial statements to evaluate profitability and decide whether to invest, hold,
or sell shares.
b) Creditors / Lenders
Banks and other lenders assess a company's ability to repay loans using balance sheets
and income statements.
c) Government & Tax Authorities
Use financial data for taxation and regulatory compliance.
d) Suppliers
Evaluate the financial stability of the business before extending credit.
e) Customers
Large clients may assess a supplier’s financial health to ensure long-term supply and
reliability.

Q2b.   Classification of Costs Based on Traceability
This classification focuses on whether a cost can be directly assigned to a specific
product, service, or department. It is divided into two types:
a) Direct Costs
Direct costs are expenses that can be specifically and easily traced to a particular
product, department, or project. These costs are directly related to the production or
service process.
Examples:
• The cost of raw materials used to manufacture a chair is a direct cost because it
is directly linked to the production of that chair.
• Wages paid to workers assembling a product in a factory are also direct costs
since they directly contribute to making the product.
b) Indirect Costs
Indirect costs are expenses that cannot be directly assigned to a specific product,
department, or project. Instead, they support overall operations and must be allocated
using an appropriate method.
Examples:
• Rent for a factory building is an indirect cost because it is used for multiple
products, and its cost must be shared among them.
•   Salaries of administrative staff who oversee multiple operations also fall under
indirect costs, as they are not linked to a specific product.
(2.5 marks each)

Q5b.   A budget is a key tool used in financial and operational planning. It provides a
roadmap for an organization to allocate resources effectively and achieve its goals.
The following are four important uses of budgeting:
•   Planning:
Budgets help in setting financial and operational targets, enabling organizations to
plan their activities in advance.
• Resource Allocation:
Budgets ensure that available resources are allocated to departments and
activities based on priorities.
• Performance Evaluation:
By comparing actual results with budgeted figures, managers can evaluate
performance and identify areas for improvement.
•   Coordination and Communication:
Budgeting promotes coordination among various departments and ensures
everyone works toward common objectives.
1.25 for each point
Q6a. Material control is an important function in inventory management, ensuring that
materials are available when needed while minimizing costs. Two widely used
techniques for material control are VED analysis and FSN analysis.
1. VED Analysis (Vital, Essential, and Desirable)
VED analysis is a technique used to classify inventory items based on their importance
in production and operations.
Classification:
Vital (V): These materials are critical for production and must always be in stock. Any
shortage can halt production and cause significant losses. Example: Important machine
spare parts, life-saving medicines in a hospital.
Essential (E): These materials are necessary for smooth operations but can be managed
with some flexibility. A short-term shortage may cause minor disruptions. Example:
Packaging materials, standard machine parts.
Desirable (D): These materials are useful but not critical. Their shortage does not
immediately affect production or operations. Example: Office stationery, decorative
items.
Purpose:
Helps prioritize inventory control based on the criticality of items.
Ensures that vital materials are never out of stock.
Reduces unnecessary stock of less critical items to control costs.
2. FSN Analysis (Fast-moving, Slow-moving, and Non-moving)
FSN analysis classifies inventory based on the speed of consumption and movement in
stock.
Classification:
Fast-moving (F): Items that are consumed quickly and need frequent replenishment.
These items have high usage rates. Example: Perishable goods like food ingredients,
commonly used raw materials.
Slow-moving (S): Items that are used occasionally and have lower consumption rates.
These need careful monitoring to avoid excess stock. Example: Special tools, seasonal
goods.
Non-moving (N): Items that have not been used for a long time. These may be obsolete
and require review to decide whether to keep or discard. Example: Old spare parts,
outdated products.
Purpose:
Ensures efficient stock management by identifying frequently used items.
Helps avoid excess stock of slow-moving and non-moving items.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Reduces storage costs and prevents wastage of obsolete materials.
Conclusion
VED Analysis focuses on the importance of materials in operations, ensuring that critical
items are always available where as FSN Analysis focuses on the speed of material
consumption, helping to maintain an optimal stock level.
Both techniques help in better material planning, reducing costs, and improving
efficiency in inventory management.

a.   1. Revenue Recognition Principle:
Definition: Revenue is recognized when it is earned and the right to receive payment is
established, not when cash is received.
Purpose: To ensure income is recorded in the correct accounting period, regardless of
when money is received.
Example: A business sells goods worth ₹1,00,000 on credit in March 2025, and receives
payment in April 2025.
According to the Revenue Recognition Principle, revenue should be recorded in March,
when the sale occurred.
2. Matching Concept:
Definition: Expenses should be recorded in the same accounting period as the
revenues they help generate.
Purpose: To correctly determine profit or loss for a specific period.
Example: If goods sold in March 2025 cost ₹60,000, this expense should also be
recorded in March 2025, even if the goods were purchased earlier.

Break-Even Point in Sales Revenue (BEP ₹)
BEP ₹=BEP units × SP=6,000×200=₹12,00,000
3 Marks (1 mark each)

Margin of Safety in Units (MOS units)

MOS ₹=MOS units × SP=4,000×200=₹8,00,000

Q2b.   Classification of Costs Based on Traceability
This classification focuses on whether a cost can be directly assigned to a specific
product, service, or department. It is divided into two types:
a) Direct Costs
Direct costs are expenses that can be specifically and easily traced to a particular
product, department, or project. These costs are directly related to the production or
service process.
Examples:
• The cost of raw materials used to manufacture a chair is a direct cost because it
is directly linked to the production of that chair.
• Wages paid to workers assembling a product in a factory are also direct costs
since they directly contribute to making the product.
b) Indirect Costs
Indirect costs are expenses that cannot be directly assigned to a specific product,
department, or project. Instead, they support overall operations and must be allocated
using an appropriate method.
Examples:
• Rent for a factory building is an indirect cost because it is used for multiple
products, and its cost must be shared among them.
•   Salaries of administrative staff who oversee multiple operations also fall under
indirect costs, as they are not linked to a specific product.
(2.5 marks each)

Q5b.   A budget is a key tool used in financial and operational planning. It provides a
roadmap for an organization to allocate resources effectively and achieve its goals.
The following are four important uses of budgeting:
•   Planning:
Budgets help in setting financial and operational targets, enabling organizations to
plan their activities in advance.
• Resource Allocation:
Budgets ensure that available resources are allocated to departments and
activities based on priorities.
• Performance Evaluation:
By comparing actual results with budgeted figures, managers can evaluate
performance and identify areas for improvement.
•   Coordination and Communication:
Budgeting promotes coordination among various departments and ensures
everyone works toward common objectives.
1.25 for each point
Q6a. Material control is an important function in inventory management, ensuring that
materials are available when needed while minimizing costs. Two widely used
techniques for material control are VED analysis and FSN analysis.
1. VED Analysis (Vital, Essential, and Desirable)
VED analysis is a technique used to classify inventory items based on their importance
in production and operations.
Classification:
Vital (V): These materials are critical for production and must always be in stock. Any
shortage can halt production and cause significant losses. Example: Important machine
spare parts, life-saving medicines in a hospital.
Essential (E): These materials are necessary for smooth operations but can be managed
with some flexibility. A short-term shortage may cause minor disruptions. Example:
Packaging materials, standard machine parts.
Desirable (D): These materials are useful but not critical. Their shortage does not
immediately affect production or operations. Example: Office stationery, decorative
items.
Purpose:
Helps prioritize inventory control based on the criticality of items.
Ensures that vital materials are never out of stock.
Reduces unnecessary stock of less critical items to control costs.
2. FSN Analysis (Fast-moving, Slow-moving, and Non-moving)
FSN analysis classifies inventory based on the speed of consumption and movement in
stock.
Classification:
Fast-moving (F): Items that are consumed quickly and need frequent replenishment.
These items have high usage rates. Example: Perishable goods like food ingredients,
commonly used raw materials.
Slow-moving (S): Items that are used occasionally and have lower consumption rates.
These need careful monitoring to avoid excess stock. Example: Special tools, seasonal
goods.
Non-moving (N): Items that have not been used for a long time. These may be obsolete
and require review to decide whether to keep or discard. Example: Old spare parts,
outdated products.
Purpose:
Ensures efficient stock management by identifying frequently used items.
Helps avoid excess stock of slow-moving and non-moving items.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Reduces storage costs and prevents wastage of obsolete materials.
Conclusion
VED Analysis focuses on the importance of materials in operations, ensuring that critical
items are always available where as FSN Analysis focuses on the speed of material
consumption, helping to maintain an optimal stock level.
Both techniques help in better material planning, reducing costs, and improving
efficiency in inventory management.

1. Labour Cost Variance (LCV)
LCV=Standard Cost−Actual Cost
=₹150,000−₹189,000=₹39,000 (Adverse)
2. Labour Rate Variance (LRV)
LRV=(Standard Rate−Actual Rate)×Actual Hours
Skilled:
(₹22−₹20)×6600=₹2×6600=₹13,200 (Favorable)
Unskilled:
(₹12−₹15)×3800=−₹3×3800=₹11,400 (Adverse)
Total LRV = ₹13,200 (F) - ₹11,400 (A) = ₹1,800 , (Favorable)
3. Labour Efficiency Variance (LEV)
LEV=(Standard Hours for Actual Output−Actual Hours)×Standard Rate
Skilled:
(5000−6600) ×₹22 =(−1600)×₹22=₹35,200 (Adverse)
Unskilled:

Q5b.   A budget is a key tool used in financial and operational planning. It provides a
roadmap for an organization to allocate resources effectively and achieve its goals.
The following are four important uses of budgeting:
•   Planning:
Budgets help in setting financial and operational targets, enabling organizations to
plan their activities in advance.
• Resource Allocation:
Budgets ensure that available resources are allocated to departments and
activities based on priorities.
• Performance Evaluation:
By comparing actual results with budgeted figures, managers can evaluate
performance and identify areas for improvement.
•   Coordination and Communication:
Budgeting promotes coordination among various departments and ensures
everyone works toward common objectives.
1.25 for each point
Q6a. Material control is an important function in inventory management, ensuring that
materials are available when needed while minimizing costs. Two widely used
techniques for material control are VED analysis and FSN analysis.
1. VED Analysis (Vital, Essential, and Desirable)
VED analysis is a technique used to classify inventory items based on their importance
in production and operations.
Classification:
Vital (V): These materials are critical for production and must always be in stock. Any
shortage can halt production and cause significant losses. Example: Important machine
spare parts, life-saving medicines in a hospital.
Essential (E): These materials are necessary for smooth operations but can be managed
with some flexibility. A short-term shortage may cause minor disruptions. Example:
Packaging materials, standard machine parts.
Desirable (D): These materials are useful but not critical. Their shortage does not
immediately affect production or operations. Example: Office stationery, decorative
items.
Purpose:
Helps prioritize inventory control based on the criticality of items.
Ensures that vital materials are never out of stock.
Reduces unnecessary stock of less critical items to control costs.
2. FSN Analysis (Fast-moving, Slow-moving, and Non-moving)
FSN analysis classifies inventory based on the speed of consumption and movement in
stock.
Classification:
Fast-moving (F): Items that are consumed quickly and need frequent replenishment.
These items have high usage rates. Example: Perishable goods like food ingredients,
commonly used raw materials.
Slow-moving (S): Items that are used occasionally and have lower consumption rates.
These need careful monitoring to avoid excess stock. Example: Special tools, seasonal
goods.
Non-moving (N): Items that have not been used for a long time. These may be obsolete
and require review to decide whether to keep or discard. Example: Old spare parts,
outdated products.
Purpose:
Ensures efficient stock management by identifying frequently used items.
Helps avoid excess stock of slow-moving and non-moving items.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Reduces storage costs and prevents wastage of obsolete materials.
Conclusion
VED Analysis focuses on the importance of materials in operations, ensuring that critical
items are always available where as FSN Analysis focuses on the speed of material
consumption, helping to maintain an optimal stock level.
Both techniques help in better material planning, reducing costs, and improving
efficiency in inventory management.

Q1   [20]
CO-1 BL-   a   Concept of Cost Accounting:   (5)
Rememb
Cost Accounting is the branch of accounting that deals with the identification, measurement,
er
allocation, aggregation, analysis, and presentation of costs of products, services, activities,
and cost centres for the purpose of planning, control, and decision-making by management.
Objectives:
1. Ascertainment of Cost: To determine the cost per unit of output, process, or service.
2. Cost Control: To compare actual costs with standards/budgets and take corrective action.
3. Cost Reduction: To achieve real and permanent reduction in cost per unit.
4. Price Fixation: To help management fix a remunerative selling price.
5. Decision-making: To provide cost data for make-or-buy, product mix, pricing, and shut-
down decisions.
Usefulness to Management:
Cost Accounting provides timely, detailed, product-wise cost data that financial accounting
cannot. It enables management to price products correctly, identify loss-making product
lines, control wastage of materials and labour, and plan for future operations.
Key Differences between Cost Accounting and Financial Accounting:
Basis   Financial Accounting   Cost Accounting
Purpose   Statutory reporting to   Cost ascertainment and
external users   control — for internal use

Rememb
A Labour Variance is the difference between the standard labour cost for actual output and
er
the actual labour cost incurred. The three principal labour variances are:
Variance   Formula   Reasons / Causes
1. Labour Cost Variance   (SH × SR) – (AH × AR)   Overall difference between
(LCV)   standard and actual labour cost.
Analyses total efficiency of labour
utilisation.
2. Labour Rate Variance   AH × (SR – AR)   Change in wage rates (union
(LRV)   settlements, overtime premium,
new labour laws, change in skill
mix). Usually uncontrollable at
supervisor level.
3. Labour Efficiency Variance SR × (SH – AH)   Difference in hours worked versus
(LEV)   standard hours for actual output.
Caused by worker skill, machine
breakdown, poor supervision,
faulty material.
Significance: Labour Rate Variance is generally a planning/personnel issue; Labour Efficiency
Variance is an operational/supervisor's responsibility. Splitting LCV into LRV and LEV enables
'Management by Exception' — only the significant variances are investigated.

Q2   [20]
CO-2 BL-   a   Classification of Costs by Controllability:   (5)
Rememb
Costs can be classified on the basis of the degree to which they can be influenced by the
er
action of a specified member of management. The classification distinguishes between costs
a manager can control and those he cannot.
1. Controllable Costs:
Costs that can be influenced by the action of a specified individual or manager during a
defined period. Usually variable costs fall in this category.
Examples: Direct materials, direct labour, power consumption, factory supplies, overtime
wages. A production foreman can control material wastage and overtime.
2. Uncontrollable (Non-controllable) Costs:
Costs that cannot be influenced by a specific manager in the short term — either because
they are fixed by contract, policy, or external factors. Usually fixed and policy costs.
Examples: Rent of factory, insurance premium, depreciation of plant, salary of the Managing
Director (at a supervisor's level), property tax.
Note: Controllability is relative — a cost uncontrollable at a foreman's level may be
controllable at the CEO's level, and what is uncontrollable in the short term may be
controllable in the long term.
Escapable vs. Inescapable Costs:
Type   Meaning   Example
Escapable Cost   Costs that can be avoided if a   Direct material cost of a
particular course of action (e.g.,   discontinued product line.
shutting down a department) is taken.
Inescapable Cost   Costs that continue to be incurred   Rent of a leased factory; salary
even if the activity is stopped   of permanent staff.
(committed costs).

CO-3 BL-   b   Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB):   (5)
Understa
Zero-Based Budgeting is a budgeting technique in which every item of expenditure must be
nd
justified from a 'zero base' each year — as though it were a brand new activity. Managers are
required to analyse their departmental needs and costs without reference to previous years'
budgets. Introduced by Peter Pyhrr (Texas Instruments, 1970) and popularised in US
government in the 1970s.
ZBB vs. Traditional (Incremental) Budgeting:
Basis   Traditional Budgeting   Zero-Based Budgeting
Starting point   Last year's actual/budgeted figures +   Zero — every rupee must be
an increment   justified afresh
Focus   Changes from previous period (inputs)   Value for money / outputs and
decision packages
Assumption   Past activities are worth continuing   No activity is continued
automatically
Effort required   Low   High — detailed cost-benefit
analysis
Advantages of ZBB: (i) eliminates wasteful expenditure, (ii) fosters cost consciousness and
critical review, (iii) links budgets to corporate objectives and priorities.
Limitations: (i) time-consuming and paperwork-intensive; (ii) requires skilled managers and
may face resistance from staff.

Given: Annual Demand (A) = 3,600 units │ Ordering Cost (O) = ₹120 per order │ Regular Price
= ₹60 per unit │ Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. = 0.25 × 60 = ₹15 per unit per year

Step 2 — Total Annual Cost Comparison (including Purchase Cost):
Cost Element   At EOQ (240 units)   At 800 units (5% discount)
Purchase price per unit   ₹ 60.00   ₹ 57.00

TOTAL ANNUAL COST   ₹ 2,19,600   ₹ 2,11,440
Annual Saving from availing discount = ₹ 2,19,600 – ₹ 2,11,440 = ₹ 8,160
Recommendation: The company SHOULD avail the 5% quantity discount. The reduction in
purchase price (₹ 10,800) more than offsets the increase in carrying cost (₹ 3,900), yielding a
net annual saving of ₹ 8,160.
CO-5 BL-   b   ABC Analysis (Always Better Control) [8 Marks]   (8)
Apply
ABC Analysis is a selective inventory control technique based on the Pareto Principle (the
80:20 rule). It classifies inventory items into three categories — A, B, and C — on the basis of
their annual consumption value (Usage × Price), and prescribes different levels of
management control and attention for each category.
Classification Pattern:
Category   % of Items   % of Value   Control Approach
A — Most important About 10% of items   About 70% of value   Tight control:
frequent review, lo
safety stock, EOQ,
strict supervision
B — Moderately   About 20% of items   About 20% of value   Moderate control:
important   periodic review,
moderate safety
stock
C — Least important About 70% of items   About 10% of value   Loose control: bulk
ordering, high safe
stock, two-bin
system
Example: An automobile assembly plant may have:
• Category A: Engine, gearbox, body panels — few in number but costly; closely monitored.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
• Category B: Tyres, batteries, wiring harness — moderate value and frequency.
• Category C: Bolts, clips, paint, fasteners — many in number but individually inexpensive;
purchased in bulk.
Benefits to the Store Manager:
1. Selective attention — management effort is focused where it matters most.
2. Reduction in investment in inventory without disrupting operations.
3. Tighter control on A-items reduces pilferage, obsolescence, and stock-outs of valuable
materials.
4. Simplifies purchasing procedures for low-value C-items (bulk buying reduces ordering cost).

CO-3 BL-   b   Master Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
Apply
A Master Budget is a comprehensive, consolidated statement that summarises all functional
(subsidiary) budgets of an organisation into one unified plan for a defined future period. It
reflects the final approved budget of the whole organisation and is typically approved at the
board level.
Components of a Master Budget:
1. Operating Budgets: Sales Budget, Production Budget, Raw Material Purchase Budget,
Direct Labour Budget, Factory Overhead Budget, Selling and Administration Budget.
2. Financial Budgets: Cash Budget, Budgeted Profit & Loss Account, Budgeted Balance Sheet,
Capital Expenditure Budget.
Significance:
• It is the primary tool of integrated corporate planning — ensures that all departments move
in the same direction.
• It quantifies the firm's goals in monetary terms and projects the financial outcome of
operations.
• It acts as the benchmark against which actual results are compared for variance analysis
and corrective action.
• It facilitates coordination between departments — the sales budget drives production,
production drives purchasing, and so on.

nd

production volume
When Will the Profits Differ?
Profits reported under the two methods differ whenever there is a change in inventory levels
(opening stock ≠ closing stock), because the fixed overheads absorbed in stock are deferred
to later periods in absorption costing but written off immediately in marginal costing.
Situation   Effect under Absorption Costing   Direction of Differenc
Production > Sales (stock ↑)   Part of fixed OH is carried forward in   Absorption profit > M
closing stock
Production < Sales (stock ↓)   Fixed OH of past periods is released in   Absorption profit < M
current period
Production = Sales (stock constant)   No deferred fixed OH   Both methods give th
Example: If fixed OH per unit is ₹ 10 and closing stock exceeds opening stock by 1,000 units,
absorption costing reports ₹ 10,000 more profit than marginal costing for the same sales.

CO-3 BL-   b   Flexible Budget – Concept and Cost Classification   (5)
Understa
A Flexible Budget is a budget prepared to show expected costs and revenues at
nd
different levels of activity or capacity utilisation. Unlike a fixed budget, which
is prepared for only one level of output, a flexible budget adjusts according to
changes in production volume, making it a more realistic and practical tool for
managerial decision-making.
It is particularly useful in industries where output levels fluctuate due to
demand variability, seasonal factors, or operational constraints.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Concept of Flexible Budget
The fundamental idea behind a flexible budget is that cost behaviour changes
with the level of activity. Therefore, instead of having a single static estimate,
multiple budgeted figures are prepared for different activity levels (e.g., 60%,
80%, 100% capacity).
This enables management to:
• Compare actual performance with the appropriate budget level
•   Control costs more effectively
• Improve planning accuracy
Classification of Costs in Flexible Budgeting
For preparing a flexible budget, costs are classified based on their behaviour
with respect to changes in activity levels:
1. Fixed Costs
• These costs remain constant in total regardless of the level of production
within a relevant range.
•   Per unit fixed cost decreases as output increases.
•   Examples: Factory rent, salaries of permanent staff, insurance.
2. Variable Costs
• These costs vary directly and proportionately with the level of output.
•   Per unit variable cost remains constant.
•   Examples: Direct materials, direct labour (in many cases), power used in
production.
3. Semi-Variable (Mixed) Costs
• These costs contain both fixed and variable components.
• They partly change with output but not in direct proportion.
•   Examples: Electricity bills (fixed charge + usage charge), maintenance costs,
telephone expenses.
Importance of Cost Classification
Proper classification of costs is critical in flexible budgeting for the following
reasons:
1. Accurate Budget Preparation
It ensures that costs are adjusted correctly according to changes in activity
levels.
2. Better Cost Control
Management can identify which costs should vary and which should remain
stable, helping in variance analysis.
3. Improved Decision-Making
Helps in decisions related to pricing, production levels, and resource
allocation.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
4. Realistic Performance Evaluation
Actual results can be compared with the budget corresponding to the actual
level of activity, avoiding misleading conclusions.
5. Efficient Resource Utilisation
Enables better planning and utilisation of resources under different operating
conditions.
Conclusion
A flexible budget is an essential tool in modern cost accounting as it reflects
the dynamic nature of business operations. The correct classification of costs
into fixed, variable, and semi-variable forms the backbone of this budgeting
technique, ensuring accuracy, control, and informed managerial decisions.
********** 13 **********
````

Q1   [20]
CO-1 BL-   a   Concept of Cost Accounting:   (5)
Rememb
Cost Accounting is the branch of accounting that deals with the identification, measurement,
er
allocation, aggregation, analysis, and presentation of costs of products, services, activities,
and cost centres for the purpose of planning, control, and decision-making by management.
Objectives:
1. Ascertainment of Cost: To determine the cost per unit of output, process, or service.
2. Cost Control: To compare actual costs with standards/budgets and take corrective action.
3. Cost Reduction: To achieve real and permanent reduction in cost per unit.
4. Price Fixation: To help management fix a remunerative selling price.
5. Decision-making: To provide cost data for make-or-buy, product mix, pricing, and shut-
down decisions.
Usefulness to Management:
Cost Accounting provides timely, detailed, product-wise cost data that financial accounting
cannot. It enables management to price products correctly, identify loss-making product
lines, control wastage of materials and labour, and plan for future operations.
Key Differences between Cost Accounting and Financial Accounting:
Basis   Financial Accounting   Cost Accounting
Purpose   Statutory reporting to   Cost ascertainment and
external users   control — for internal use

A Labour Variance is the difference between the standard labour cost for actual output and
er

Variance   Formula   Reasons / Causes

Q2   [20]
CO-2 BL-   a   Classification of Costs by Controllability:   (5)
Rememb
Costs can be classified on the basis of the degree to which they can be influenced by the
er
action of a specified member of management. The classification distinguishes between costs
a manager can control and those he cannot.
1. Controllable Costs:
Costs that can be influenced by the action of a specified individual or manager during a
defined period. Usually variable costs fall in this category.
Examples: Direct materials, direct labour, power consumption, factory supplies, overtime
wages. A production foreman can control material wastage and overtime.
2. Uncontrollable (Non-controllable) Costs:
Costs that cannot be influenced by a specific manager in the short term — either because
they are fixed by contract, policy, or external factors. Usually fixed and policy costs.
Examples: Rent of factory, insurance premium, depreciation of plant, salary of the Managing
Director (at a supervisor's level), property tax.
Note: Controllability is relative — a cost uncontrollable at a foreman's level may be
controllable at the CEO's level, and what is uncontrollable in the short term may be
controllable in the long term.
Escapable vs. Inescapable Costs:
Type   Meaning   Example
Escapable Cost   Costs that can be avoided if a   Direct material cost of a
particular course of action (e.g.,   discontinued product line.
shutting down a department) is taken.
Inescapable Cost   Costs that continue to be incurred   Rent of a leased factory; salary
even if the activity is stopped   of permanent staff.
(committed costs).

CO-3 BL-   b   Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB):   (5)
Understa
Zero-Based Budgeting is a budgeting technique in which every item of expenditure must be
nd
justified from a 'zero base' each year — as though it were a brand new activity. Managers are
required to analyse their departmental needs and costs without reference to previous years'
budgets. Introduced by Peter Pyhrr (Texas Instruments, 1970) and popularised in US
government in the 1970s.
ZBB vs. Traditional (Incremental) Budgeting:
Basis   Traditional Budgeting   Zero-Based Budgeting
Starting point   Last year's actual/budgeted figures +   Zero — every rupee must be
an increment   justified afresh
Focus   Changes from previous period (inputs)   Value for money / outputs and
decision packages
Assumption   Past activities are worth continuing   No activity is continued
automatically
Effort required   Low   High — detailed cost-benefit
analysis
Advantages of ZBB: (i) eliminates wasteful expenditure, (ii) fosters cost consciousness and
critical review, (iii) links budgets to corporate objectives and priorities.
Limitations: (i) time-consuming and paperwork-intensive; (ii) requires skilled managers and
may face resistance from staff.

Apply

Given: Annual Demand (A) = 3,600 units │ Ordering Cost (O) = ₹120 per order │ Regular Price
= ₹60 per unit │ Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. = 0.25 × 60 = ₹15 per unit per year
Supplier offers 5% discount if order size ≥ 800 units → Discounted Price = ₹60 × 0.95 =
₹57/unit; Carrying Cost at discounted price = 0.25 × 57 = ₹14.25/unit
Step 1 — EOQ at the Regular Price:
EOQ = √(2 × A × O ÷ C) = √(2 × 3,600 × 120 ÷ 15) = √(8,64,000 ÷ 15) = √57,600 = 240 units ✓
Step 2 — Total Annual Cost Comparison (including Purchase Cost):
Cost Element   At EOQ (240 units)   At 800 units (5% discount)
Purchase price per unit   ₹ 60.00   ₹ 57.00
No. of orders per year   3,600 ÷ 240 = 15 orders   3,600 ÷ 800 = 4.5 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   15 × 120 = ₹ 1,800   4.5 × 120 = ₹ 540
Average Inventory   240 ÷ 2 = 120 units   800 ÷ 2 = 400 units
Annual Carrying Cost   120 × ₹15 = ₹ 1,800   400 × ₹14.25 = ₹ 5,700
Annual Purchase Cost   3,600 × 60 = ₹ 2,16,000   3,600 × 57 = ₹ 2,05,200
TOTAL ANNUAL COST   ₹ 2,19,600   ₹ 2,11,440
Annual Saving from availing discount = ₹ 2,19,600 – ₹ 2,11,440 = ₹ 8,160
Recommendation: The company SHOULD avail the 5% quantity discount. The reduction in
purchase price (₹ 10,800) more than offsets the increase in carrying cost (₹ 3,900), yielding a
net annual saving of ₹ 8,160.
CO-5 BL-   b   ABC Analysis (Always Better Control) [8 Marks]   (8)
Apply
ABC Analysis is a selective inventory control technique based on the Pareto Principle (the
80:20 rule). It classifies inventory items into three categories — A, B, and C — on the basis of
their annual consumption value (Usage × Price), and prescribes different levels of
management control and attention for each category.
Classification Pattern:
Category   % of Items   % of Value   Control Approach
A — Most important About 10% of items   About 70% of value   Tight control:
frequent review, lo
safety stock, EOQ,
strict supervision
B — Moderately   About 20% of items   About 20% of value   Moderate control:
important   periodic review,
moderate safety
stock
C — Least important About 70% of items   About 10% of value   Loose control: bulk
ordering, high safe
stock, two-bin
system
Example: An automobile assembly plant may have:
• Category A: Engine, gearbox, body panels — few in number but costly; closely monitored.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
• Category B: Tyres, batteries, wiring harness — moderate value and frequency.
• Category C: Bolts, clips, paint, fasteners — many in number but individually inexpensive;
purchased in bulk.
Benefits to the Store Manager:
1. Selective attention — management effort is focused where it matters most.
2. Reduction in investment in inventory without disrupting operations.
3. Tighter control on A-items reduces pilferage, obsolescence, and stock-outs of valuable
materials.
4. Simplifies purchasing procedures for low-value C-items (bulk buying reduces ordering cost).

CO-3 BL-   b   Master Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
Apply
A Master Budget is a comprehensive, consolidated statement that summarises all functional
(subsidiary) budgets of an organisation into one unified plan for a defined future period. It
reflects the final approved budget of the whole organisation and is typically approved at the
board level.
Components of a Master Budget:
1. Operating Budgets: Sales Budget, Production Budget, Raw Material Purchase Budget,
Direct Labour Budget, Factory Overhead Budget, Selling and Administration Budget.
2. Financial Budgets: Cash Budget, Budgeted Profit & Loss Account, Budgeted Balance Sheet,
Capital Expenditure Budget.
Significance:
• It is the primary tool of integrated corporate planning — ensures that all departments move
in the same direction.
• It quantifies the firm's goals in monetary terms and projects the financial outcome of
operations.
• It acts as the benchmark against which actual results are compared for variance analysis
and corrective action.
• It facilitates coordination between departments — the sales budget drives production,
production drives purchasing, and so on.

Basis   Marginal Costing   Absorption Costing
nd

analysis, pricing   AS/Ind AS)
Effect on Profit   Profit depends only on sales volume   Profit depends on both sales and
production volume
When Will the Profits Differ?
Profits reported under the two methods differ whenever there is a change in inventory levels
(opening stock ≠ closing stock), because the fixed overheads absorbed in stock are deferred
to later periods in absorption costing but written off immediately in marginal costing.
Situation   Effect under Absorption Costing   Direction of Differenc
Production > Sales (stock ↑)   Part of fixed OH is carried forward in   Absorption profit > M
closing stock
Production < Sales (stock ↓)   Fixed OH of past periods is released in   Absorption profit < M
current period
Production = Sales (stock constant)   No deferred fixed OH   Both methods give th
Example: If fixed OH per unit is ₹ 10 and closing stock exceeds opening stock by 1,000 units,
absorption costing reports ₹ 10,000 more profit than marginal costing for the same sales.

CO-3 BL-   b   Flexible Budget – Concept and Cost Classification   (5)
Understa
A Flexible Budget is a budget prepared to show expected costs and revenues at
nd
different levels of activity or capacity utilisation. Unlike a fixed budget, which
is prepared for only one level of output, a flexible budget adjusts according to
changes in production volume, making it a more realistic and practical tool for
managerial decision-making.
It is particularly useful in industries where output levels fluctuate due to
demand variability, seasonal factors, or operational constraints.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Concept of Flexible Budget
The fundamental idea behind a flexible budget is that cost behaviour changes
with the level of activity. Therefore, instead of having a single static estimate,
multiple budgeted figures are prepared for different activity levels (e.g., 60%,
80%, 100% capacity).
This enables management to:
• Compare actual performance with the appropriate budget level
•   Control costs more effectively
• Improve planning accuracy
Classification of Costs in Flexible Budgeting
For preparing a flexible budget, costs are classified based on their behaviour
with respect to changes in activity levels:
1. Fixed Costs
• These costs remain constant in total regardless of the level of production
within a relevant range.
•   Per unit fixed cost decreases as output increases.
•   Examples: Factory rent, salaries of permanent staff, insurance.
2. Variable Costs
• These costs vary directly and proportionately with the level of output.
•   Per unit variable cost remains constant.
•   Examples: Direct materials, direct labour (in many cases), power used in
production.
3. Semi-Variable (Mixed) Costs
• These costs contain both fixed and variable components.
• They partly change with output but not in direct proportion.
•   Examples: Electricity bills (fixed charge + usage charge), maintenance costs,
telephone expenses.
Importance of Cost Classification
Proper classification of costs is critical in flexible budgeting for the following
reasons:
1. Accurate Budget Preparation
It ensures that costs are adjusted correctly according to changes in activity
levels.
2. Better Cost Control
Management can identify which costs should vary and which should remain
stable, helping in variance analysis.
3. Improved Decision-Making
Helps in decisions related to pricing, production levels, and resource
allocation.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
4. Realistic Performance Evaluation
Actual results can be compared with the budget corresponding to the actual
level of activity, avoiding misleading conclusions.
5. Efficient Resource Utilisation
Enables better planning and utilisation of resources under different operating
conditions.
Conclusion
A flexible budget is an essential tool in modern cost accounting as it reflects
the dynamic nature of business operations. The correct classification of costs
into fixed, variable, and semi-variable forms the backbone of this budgeting
technique, ensuring accuracy, control, and informed managerial decisions.
********** 13 **********
````

Q1   [20]
CO-1 BL-   a   Concept of Cost Accounting:   (5)
Rememb
Cost Accounting is the branch of accounting that deals with the identification, measurement,
er
allocation, aggregation, analysis, and presentation of costs of products, services, activities,
and cost centres for the purpose of planning, control, and decision-making by management.
Objectives:
1. Ascertainment of Cost: To determine the cost per unit of output, process, or service.
2. Cost Control: To compare actual costs with standards/budgets and take corrective action.
3. Cost Reduction: To achieve real and permanent reduction in cost per unit.
4. Price Fixation: To help management fix a remunerative selling price.
5. Decision-making: To provide cost data for make-or-buy, product mix, pricing, and shut-
down decisions.
Usefulness to Management:
Cost Accounting provides timely, detailed, product-wise cost data that financial accounting
cannot. It enables management to price products correctly, identify loss-making product
lines, control wastage of materials and labour, and plan for future operations.
Key Differences between Cost Accounting and Financial Accounting:
Basis   Financial Accounting   Cost Accounting
Purpose   Statutory reporting to   Cost ascertainment and
external users   control — for internal use

and Balance Sheet   process-wise, department-

Companies Act   specified industries

A Labour Variance is the difference between the standard labour cost for actual output and
er

Variance   Formula   Reasons / Causes

Q2   [20]
CO-2 BL-   a   Classification of Costs by Controllability:   (5)
Rememb
Costs can be classified on the basis of the degree to which they can be influenced by the
er
action of a specified member of management. The classification distinguishes between costs
a manager can control and those he cannot.
1. Controllable Costs:
Costs that can be influenced by the action of a specified individual or manager during a
defined period. Usually variable costs fall in this category.
Examples: Direct materials, direct labour, power consumption, factory supplies, overtime
wages. A production foreman can control material wastage and overtime.
2. Uncontrollable (Non-controllable) Costs:
Costs that cannot be influenced by a specific manager in the short term — either because
they are fixed by contract, policy, or external factors. Usually fixed and policy costs.
Examples: Rent of factory, insurance premium, depreciation of plant, salary of the Managing
Director (at a supervisor's level), property tax.
Note: Controllability is relative — a cost uncontrollable at a foreman's level may be
controllable at the CEO's level, and what is uncontrollable in the short term may be
controllable in the long term.
Escapable vs. Inescapable Costs:
Type   Meaning   Example
Escapable Cost   Costs that can be avoided if a   Direct material cost of a
particular course of action (e.g.,   discontinued product line.
shutting down a department) is taken.
Inescapable Cost   Costs that continue to be incurred   Rent of a leased factory; salary
even if the activity is stopped   of permanent staff.
(committed costs).

CO-3 BL-   b   Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB):   (5)
Understa
Zero-Based Budgeting is a budgeting technique in which every item of expenditure must be
nd
justified from a 'zero base' each year — as though it were a brand new activity. Managers are
required to analyse their departmental needs and costs without reference to previous years'
budgets. Introduced by Peter Pyhrr (Texas Instruments, 1970) and popularised in US
government in the 1970s.
ZBB vs. Traditional (Incremental) Budgeting:
Basis   Traditional Budgeting   Zero-Based Budgeting
Starting point   Last year's actual/budgeted figures +   Zero — every rupee must be
an increment   justified afresh
Focus   Changes from previous period (inputs)   Value for money / outputs and
decision packages
Assumption   Past activities are worth continuing   No activity is continued
automatically
Effort required   Low   High — detailed cost-benefit
analysis
Advantages of ZBB: (i) eliminates wasteful expenditure, (ii) fosters cost consciousness and
critical review, (iii) links budgets to corporate objectives and priorities.
Limitations: (i) time-consuming and paperwork-intensive; (ii) requires skilled managers and
may face resistance from staff.

Step 2 — Total Annual Cost Comparison (including Purchase Cost):
Cost Element   At EOQ (240 units)   At 800 units (5% discount)
Purchase price per unit   ₹ 60.00   ₹ 57.00

Recommendation: The company SHOULD avail the 5% quantity discount. The reduction in
purchase price (₹ 10,800) more than offsets the increase in carrying cost (₹ 3,900), yielding a
net annual saving of ₹ 8,160.
CO-5 BL-   b   ABC Analysis (Always Better Control) [8 Marks]   (8)
Apply
ABC Analysis is a selective inventory control technique based on the Pareto Principle (the
80:20 rule). It classifies inventory items into three categories — A, B, and C — on the basis of
their annual consumption value (Usage × Price), and prescribes different levels of
management control and attention for each category.
Classification Pattern:
Category   % of Items   % of Value   Control Approach
A — Most important About 10% of items   About 70% of value   Tight control:
frequent review, lo
safety stock, EOQ,
strict supervision
B — Moderately   About 20% of items   About 20% of value   Moderate control:
important   periodic review,
moderate safety
stock
C — Least important About 70% of items   About 10% of value   Loose control: bulk
ordering, high safe
stock, two-bin
system
Example: An automobile assembly plant may have:
• Category A: Engine, gearbox, body panels — few in number but costly; closely monitored.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
CO / BL   Par   Question   Mar
t   ks
• Category B: Tyres, batteries, wiring harness — moderate value and frequency.
• Category C: Bolts, clips, paint, fasteners — many in number but individually inexpensive;
purchased in bulk.
Benefits to the Store Manager:
1. Selective attention — management effort is focused where it matters most.
2. Reduction in investment in inventory without disrupting operations.
3. Tighter control on A-items reduces pilferage, obsolescence, and stock-outs of valuable
materials.
4. Simplifies purchasing procedures for low-value C-items (bulk buying reduces ordering cost).

CO-3 BL-   b   Master Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
Apply
A Master Budget is a comprehensive, consolidated statement that summarises all functional
(subsidiary) budgets of an organisation into one unified plan for a defined future period. It
reflects the final approved budget of the whole organisation and is typically approved at the
board level.
Components of a Master Budget:
1. Operating Budgets: Sales Budget, Production Budget, Raw Material Purchase Budget,
Direct Labour Budget, Factory Overhead Budget, Selling and Administration Budget.
2. Financial Budgets: Cash Budget, Budgeted Profit & Loss Account, Budgeted Balance Sheet,
Capital Expenditure Budget.
Significance:
• It is the primary tool of integrated corporate planning — ensures that all departments move
in the same direction.
• It quantifies the firm's goals in monetary terms and projects the financial outcome of
operations.
• It acts as the benchmark against which actual results are compared for variance analysis
and corrective action.
• It facilitates coordination between departments — the sales budget drives production,
production drives purchasing, and so on.

CO-3 BL-   b   Marginal Costing vs. Absorption Costing [8 Marks]   (8)
Understa
Basis   Marginal Costing   Absorption Costing
nd
Treatment of Fixed OH   Treated as a period cost — charged in Treated as a product cost — include
full to the P&L of the period   in cost of production and inventory
Inventory Valuation   Valued at variable cost only   Valued at total (variable + fixed)
factory cost
Cost Statement Focus   Contribution (Sales – Variable Cost)   Gross Profit (Sales – Cost of Goods
Sold)
Purpose   Short-term decision-making, CVP   Statutory reporting (required under
analysis, pricing   AS/Ind AS)
Effect on Profit   Profit depends only on sales volume   Profit depends on both sales and
production volume
When Will the Profits Differ?
Profits reported under the two methods differ whenever there is a change in inventory levels
(opening stock ≠ closing stock), because the fixed overheads absorbed in stock are deferred
to later periods in absorption costing but written off immediately in marginal costing.
Situation   Effect under Absorption Costing   Direction of Differenc
Production > Sales (stock ↑)   Part of fixed OH is carried forward in   Absorption profit > M
closing stock
Production < Sales (stock ↓)   Fixed OH of past periods is released in   Absorption profit < M
current period
Production = Sales (stock constant)   No deferred fixed OH   Both methods give th
Example: If fixed OH per unit is ₹ 10 and closing stock exceeds opening stock by 1,000 units,
absorption costing reports ₹ 10,000 more profit than marginal costing for the same sales.
