CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********

Q1   [20]
CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

CO-1   c   Definition: Management Accounting is the process of identification, measurement, accumulation,   (5)
BL-   analysis, preparation, interpretation, and communication of financial information used by
management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and
Rememb
accountability for its resources.
er
Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.
Basis   Cost Accounting   Management Accounting
Scope   Restricted to cost ascertainment   Wider — includes financial and cost
data
Objective   Cost control and cost reduction   Overall management decision-
making
Techniques   Cost sheets, Job costing, Process   Budgeting, Marginal costing, Ratio
costing   analysis
Obligation   Not mandatory but widely used   Not mandatory; purely for internal
use

Q2   [20]
CO-2   a   1. Material Cost: The cost of all materials used in the manufacturing process. Direct Materials (e.g.,   (5)
BL-   raw materials directly identifiable in the finished product — iron ore in steel) form part of Prime Cost.
Indirect Materials (e.g., lubricants, cotton waste, small tools) are part of Factory Overheads.
Rememb
2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

MATERIAL VARIANCES:
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result

(MCV)   (25,000×14.50) = 3,60,000 –
3,62,500

(MUV)   (–1,000)

CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********
````

Q1   [20]
CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

CO-1   c   Definition: Management Accounting is the process of identification, measurement, accumulation,   (5)
BL-   analysis, preparation, interpretation, and communication of financial information used by
management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and
Rememb
accountability for its resources.
er
Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.
Basis   Cost Accounting   Management Accounting
Scope   Restricted to cost ascertainment   Wider — includes financial and cost
data
Objective   Cost control and cost reduction   Overall management decision-
making
Techniques   Cost sheets, Job costing, Process   Budgeting, Marginal costing, Ratio
costing   analysis
Obligation   Not mandatory but widely used   Not mandatory; purely for internal
use

CO-2   a   1. Material Cost: The cost of all materials used in the manufacturing process. Direct Materials (e.g.,   (5)
BL-   raw materials directly identifiable in the finished product — iron ore in steel) form part of Prime Cost.
Indirect Materials (e.g., lubricants, cotton waste, small tools) are part of Factory Overheads.
Rememb
2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

MATERIAL VARIANCES:
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result

(MCV)   (25,000×14.50) = 3,60,000 –
3,62,500

(MUV)   (–1,000)
Material Price Variance   AQ × (SR – AR)   25,000 × (15 – 14.50) =   ₹ 12,500 (F)
(MPV)   25,000 × 0.50
Verification: MCV = MUV + MPV → 2,500(A) = 15,000(A) – 12,500(F) ✓
LABOUR VARIANCES:
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result
Labour Cost Variance (LCV)   (SH × SR) – (AH × AR)   (32,000×30) – (30,500×31) =   ₹ 14,500 (F)
9,60,000 – 9,45,500

(LEV)   1,500

CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

to the fixed cost base being already optimal.
CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********
````

Q1   [20]
CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

BL-   analysis, preparation, interpretation, and communication of financial information used by
management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and
Rememb

er
Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.

CO-2   a   1. Material Cost: The cost of all materials used in the manufacturing process. Direct Materials (e.g.,   (5)
BL-   raw materials directly identifiable in the finished product — iron ore in steel) form part of Prime Cost.
Indirect Materials (e.g., lubricants, cotton waste, small tools) are part of Factory Overheads.
Rememb
2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

MATERIAL VARIANCES:

(MCV)   (25,000×14.50) = 3,60,000 –
3,62,500

(MUV)   (–1,000)

********** 7 **********

9,60,000 – 9,45,500

CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********
````

CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and

Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.

CO-2   a   1. Material Cost: The cost of all materials used in the manufacturing process. Direct Materials (e.g.,   (5)
BL-   raw materials directly identifiable in the finished product — iron ore in steel) form part of Prime Cost.
Indirect Materials (e.g., lubricants, cotton waste, small tools) are part of Factory Overheads.
Rememb
2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

Actual Rate (Labour) = ₹9,45,500 ÷ 30,500 = ₹ 31.00 per hour
MATERIAL VARIANCES:
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result

(MCV)   (25,000×14.50) = 3,60,000 –
3,62,500
Material Usage Variance   SR × (SQ – AQ)   15 × (24,000 – 25,000) = 15 × ₹ 15,000 (A)
(MUV)   (–1,000)

(MPV)   25,000 × 0.50
Verification: MCV = MUV + MPV → 2,500(A) = 15,000(A) – 12,500(F) ✓
LABOUR VARIANCES:
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result
Labour Cost Variance (LCV)   (SH × SR) – (AH × AR)   (32,000×30) – (30,500×31) =   ₹ 14,500 (F)
9,60,000 – 9,45,500

(LEV)   1,500

CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********
````

CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and

Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.

2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

BL-Apply   Given: Standard: Material 3 kg @ ₹15/kg; Labour 4 hrs @ ₹30/hr. Actual: 8,000 units produced.
Step 1 — Standard Quantities for Actual Output:

Actual Rate (Material) = ₹3,62,500 ÷ 25,000 = ₹ 14.50 per kg
Actual Rate (Labour) = ₹9,45,500 ÷ 30,500 = ₹ 31.00 per hour
MATERIAL VARIANCES:
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result

(MCV)   (25,000×14.50) = 3,60,000 –
3,62,500

(MUV)   (–1,000)

(MPV)   25,000 × 0.50

********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result

9,60,000 – 9,45,500

(LEV)   1,500

Verification: LCV = LEV + LRV → 14,500(F) = 45,000(F) – 30,500(A) ✓
CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********
````

Q1   [20]
CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

BL-   analysis, preparation, interpretation, and communication of financial information used by
management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and

Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.

CO-2   a   1. Material Cost: The cost of all materials used in the manufacturing process. Direct Materials (e.g.,   (5)
BL-   raw materials directly identifiable in the finished product — iron ore in steel) form part of Prime Cost.
Indirect Materials (e.g., lubricants, cotton waste, small tools) are part of Factory Overheads.
Rememb
2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

CO-4   a   (a) Material and Labour Variances [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Standard: Material 3 kg @ ₹15/kg; Labour 4 hrs @ ₹30/hr. Actual: 8,000 units produced.
Step 1 — Standard Quantities for Actual Output:

Actual Rate (Labour) = ₹9,45,500 ÷ 30,500 = ₹ 31.00 per hour
MATERIAL VARIANCES:
Variance   Formula   Calculation   Result

(MCV)   (25,000×14.50) = 3,60,000 –
3,62,500

(MUV)   (–1,000)

********** 7 **********

9,60,000 – 9,45,500

(LEV)   1,500
Labour Rate Variance (LRV)   AH × (SR – AR)   30,500 × (30 – 31) = 30,500 × (–1)   ₹ 30,500 (A)
Verification: LCV = LEV + LRV → 14,500(F) = 45,000(F) – 30,500(A) ✓
CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********

Q1   [20]
CO-1   a   Accounting has three primary branches:   (5)
BL-   1. Financial Accounting: Records all financial transactions systematically (journals, ledger, trial
Rememb   balance, and final accounts). It is meant for EXTERNAL users such as investors, banks, creditors,
and government. It presents the overall financial position (Balance Sheet) and profitability (P&L
er
Account) of the organisation.
2. Cost Accounting: Ascertains, records, and analyses the cost of products, processes, and
services for INTERNAL management. It helps in cost control, cost reduction, price fixation, and
budgeting. It provides a detailed cost sheet covering prime cost, works cost, cost of production, cost
of goods sold, and cost of sales.
3. Management Accounting: Uses data from both financial and cost accounting to present
information in a form useful to management for planning, decision-making, and control. Techniques
include budgetary control, marginal costing, standard costing, ratio analysis, and fund flow analysis.
It is forward-looking, flexible, and not legally mandated.
Significance: Each branch serves a specific purpose — financial accounting for statutory reporting,
cost accounting for product-level cost control, and management accounting for strategic decision-
making.

CO-1   c   Definition: Management Accounting is the process of identification, measurement, accumulation,   (5)
BL-   analysis, preparation, interpretation, and communication of financial information used by
management to plan, evaluate, and control within an entity, and to assure appropriate use and
Rememb
accountability for its resources.
er
Objectives of Management Accounting:
1. Planning and Forecasting: Helps prepare budgets and forecasts for future periods.
2. Decision-Making: Provides relevant cost and revenue data for operational and strategic
decisions (make-or-buy, pricing, product mix).
3. Control: Enables comparison of actual results with budgeted figures and initiates corrective
action.
4. Communication: Presents information to managers at all levels for coordinated action.
5. Performance Evaluation: Measures efficiency of departments and individuals using variance
analysis and ratio analysis.

data

making

costing   analysis

CO-2   a   1. Material Cost: The cost of all materials used in the manufacturing process. Direct Materials (e.g.,   (5)
BL-   raw materials directly identifiable in the finished product — iron ore in steel) form part of Prime Cost.
Indirect Materials (e.g., lubricants, cotton waste, small tools) are part of Factory Overheads.
Rememb
2. Labour Cost: The cost of human effort employed in production. Direct Labour (Productive Wages
er
— e.g., wages of machine operators directly engaged in production) forms part of Prime Cost. Indirect
Labour (e.g., salary of factory supervisor, storekeeper) is part of Factory Overheads.
3. Expenses / Overheads: All other costs apart from materials and labour. These are classified as:
(i) Factory Overheads — Factory rent, power, depreciation on plant; (ii) Office & Administration
Overheads — Office rent, Director's fees, audit fees; (iii) Selling & Distribution Overheads —
Advertisement, carriage outwards, salesman's commission.

Q4   [20]
CO-2   a   Economic Order Quantity (EOQ) & Savings [12 Marks]   (12)
BL-Apply   Given: Current lot = 500 units (3-month supply) → Annual Demand = 500 × 4 = 2,000 units
Ordering Cost (O) = ₹ 100 per order | Price per unit = ₹ 40 | Carrying Cost = 25% p.a. per unit =
40 × 25% = ₹ 10 per unit per year
Step 1 — EOQ Formula:
EOQ = √( 2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Carrying Cost per unit )
EOQ = √( 2 × 2,000 × 100 ÷ 10 ) = √( 4,00,000 ÷ 10 ) = √40,000 = 200 units ✓
Step 2 — Annual Cost Comparison:
Cost Element   Current (500 units)   At EOQ (200 units)
No. of orders per year   2,000 ÷ 500 = 4 orders   2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Annual Ordering Cost   4 × ₹100 = ₹ 400   10 × ₹100 = ₹ 1,000
Average Stock (Avg Inv.)   500 ÷ 2 = 250 units   200 ÷ 2 = 100 units
Annual Carrying Cost   250 × ₹10 = ₹ 2,500   100 × ₹10 = ₹ 1,000
Total Annual Inventory Cost   ₹ 400 + ₹ 2,500 = ₹ 2,900   ₹ 1,000 + ₹ 1,000 = ₹ 2,000
Annual Savings = ₹ 2,900 – ₹ 2,000 = ₹ 900 per year
Step 3 — Orders and Time Gap at EOQ:
No. of orders per year = 2,000 ÷ 200 = 10 orders
Time gap between orders = 365 ÷ 10 = 36.5 days ≈ 37 days
CO-5   b   Cost-Volume-Profit (C-V-P) Analysis [8 Marks]   (8)
BL-Apply   Definition: CVP Analysis (also called Break-Even Analysis) is a technique that examines the
relationship between changes in costs (fixed and variable), volume (level of output or sales), and profit.
It helps management understand how changes in output level affect the company's costs and profits.
Key Components:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Component   Formula / Explanation
Contribution   Sales – Variable Cost (also = Fixed Cost + Profit)
P/V Ratio   Contribution ÷ Sales × 100 (measures profit earned per ₹ of sales)
Break-Even Point (BEP)   Fixed Cost ÷ P/V Ratio (in ₹) OR Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution per unit
(in units)
Margin of Safety (MOS)   Actual Sales – Break-Even Sales (excess of actual sales over BEP)
Sales for Target Profit   (Fixed Cost + Desired Profit) ÷ P/V Ratio
Key Assumptions:
1. All costs can be separated into fixed and variable components.
2. Fixed costs remain constant within the relevant range of output.
3. Variable cost per unit remains constant at all levels of activity.
4. Selling price per unit is constant (no discounts for higher volumes).
5. Production equals sales — no change in opening/closing stock.
6. Only one product is produced, or the sales mix remains constant.
Usefulness for Management Decision-Making:
1. Profit Planning: Determines the sales level needed to earn a target profit.
2. Price Fixation: Helps set a minimum selling price to cover variable cost and contribute towards
fixed cost.
3. Product Mix Decisions: Identifies which product gives the highest P/V ratio when resources are
limited.
4. Make-or-Buy Decisions: Compares marginal cost of making vs. buying a component.
5. Advertising Decisions: Evaluates how much additional sales are needed to justify an increase
in advertising spend.

CO-3   b   (b) Flexible Budget vs. Fixed Budget [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-Apply   A Flexible Budget is a budget designed to change in accordance with the actual level of activity or
output achieved. It provides targets at different capacity levels and serves as a more realistic
benchmark for cost control.
Basis   Fixed Budget   Flexible Budget
Activity Level   Based on one level of output   Prepared for multiple output levels
Cost Behaviour   Does not distinguish between fixed   Explicitly separates fixed, variable,
and variable costs   and semi-variable costs
Usefulness for Control   Less useful when actual output   More useful — actual costs
differs from budget   compared at the actual output
level
Applicability   Suitable when output is predictable   Suitable for businesses with
and stable   fluctuating demand
Variance Analysis   Overall variance may be   Variances are more meaningful
misleading   and actionable

CO-3   b   Definition: Standard Costing is a technique of cost accounting in which pre-determined (standard)   (8)
BL-   costs are established for materials, labour, and overheads, and actual costs are compared with these
standards to analyse variances and initiate corrective action.
Understa
Steps in Establishing a Standard Costing System:
nd
1. Classification and Codification of Accounts: All cost elements are identified, classified, and
coded.
2. Setting Material Standards: Standard price, quality, and quantity of material per unit of output
are determined based on historical data and market surveys.
3. Setting Labour Standards: Standard time (hours) per operation and standard wage rates are
fixed through time-and-motion studies.
4. Setting Overhead Standards: Predetermined overhead absorption rates are established for
factory, administration, and selling overheads based on budgeted output.
5. Cost Card Preparation: A standard cost card is prepared for each product summarising all
standard costs.
6. Actual Cost Recording: Actual costs incurred are recorded systematically.
7. Variance Analysis: Differences (variances) between standard and actual costs are computed
and analysed (MCV, LCV, OAV, etc.).
8. Reporting and Corrective Action: Variances are reported to management for investigation and
corrective measures.
Advantages: Provides a basis for performance evaluation, promotes cost consciousness, simplifies
stock valuation, and aids pricing and budgetary control.

CO-3   b   (b) Advantages of Budgeting [5 Marks]   (5)
BL-   Budgeting is the process of preparing quantitative plans (budgets) for a specific future period,
Understa   covering revenues, costs, and resources required to achieve organisational objectives.
nd   Advantages of Budgeting for a Business Organisation:
1. Planning: Compels management to think ahead systematically. Potential problems are
identified in advance and plans are made to address them before they arise.
2. Co-ordination: Ensures that all departments (Production, Sales, Finance, HR) work in harmony
towards common organisational goals. Interdepartmental conflicts are minimised.
3. Control: Provides a benchmark against which actual performance is measured. Variances
between budgeted and actual figures are identified and corrective action is taken promptly.
4. Communication: Clearly communicates the organisation's plans and expectations to managers
at all levels, ensuring everyone understands their targets and responsibilities.
5. Motivation: Sets measurable targets for employees and departments. Budgets act as
performance incentives and encourage managers to achieve their goals efficiently.
6. Resource Allocation: Ensures that financial and physical resources are allocated optimally to
departments and activities that contribute most to the organisation's objectives.
7. Performance Evaluation: Provides an objective basis for evaluating the performance of
departments and individual managers by comparing actual results with budgeted figures.
********** 9 **********

Q1
a. Money measurement concept
•   Only transactions measurable in money are recorded in accounting.
• Non-monetary factors (e.g., employee skill, goodwill unless purchased) are excluded.
•   Practical effect: financial statements reflect monetary data only, enabling aggregation and
comparison.
Materiality concept
• An item is material if its omission or misstatement could influence economic decisions of users.
•   Small / immaterial items can be aggregated or omitted; materiality depends on relative size and
context.   (2.5 marks each)
b.   •   Selling price per unit = ₹50.
•   Marginal cost per unit = 60% of ₹50 = 0.60 × 50 = 30.
•   Contribution per unit = Selling price − Variable cost = 50 − 30 = 20
•   Fixed Cost: ₹5,00,000
•   Production: 50,000 units

Margin of Safety (units) = Actual units − BEP units = 50,000 − 25,000 = 25,000 units.   1 mark
Margin of Safety (%) = (25,000 ÷ 50,000) × 100 = 50%.   1 mark

d.   Importance and limitations of financial accounting
Importance
•   Records financial transactions systematically.
•   Provides basis for decision making, budgeting and control.
• Produces financial statements for stakeholders (creditors, investors, management).
•   Facilitates legal compliance and tax computation.
Limitations
• Money measurement excludes non-monetary factors (e.g., employee skill).
•   Historic cost convention may not reflect current market values.
•   Subject to estimates and judgments (depreciation, provisions).
• May be affected by window dressing or aggressive accounting.   2.5 marks each
